summary: Choose checks that detect the changed behavior's plausible failures while permitting internal restructuring. For refactoring, establish the behavior to preserve and retain meaningful assertions across the change.
kind: reference
scope: Software changes and refactoring; test selection depends on observable contracts, integration risk and execution cost
keywords: [behavior testing, regression, refactoring, test boundary, test pyramid, contract test, 회귀 테스트, 리팩터링, 동작 검증, 테스트 경계]
status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-07'
context:
  evidence_check: Original Google testing article and Martin Fowler guidance inspected online
  application_status: Proposed verification method; no project tests or controlled mutations executed
---

# Verify changes at a stable behavior boundary

## Guidance

Start from a concrete obligation: which result, failure, side effect or resource transition must a caller observe? Choose a boundary that exposes that obligation while leaving irrelevant implementation decisions free to change. A unit may contain cooperating functions or classes, beyond one private method.

Proposed method: derive expected outcomes from the requirement, contract, independently known example or invariant. Avoid reproducing the production calculation in the assertion. Control time, randomness and external responses when they would obscure the failure being checked. Use a real integration where a substitute cannot establish the relevant compatibility.

For a behavior-preserving refactoring, establish a passing baseline and identify the assertions expected to remain valid. Change in reviewable steps and rerun affected checks. A constructor or fixture adjustment may be necessary; changing an expected result deserves investigation. For an intentional behavior change, state the new expectation separately from the behavior that must remain compatible.

## Applicability and exceptions

Apply this approach to features, consequential fixes, dependency changes and restructuring. Choose focused or broad tests according to actual risk, speed, reliability and maintenance cost; no fixed test count or universal layer ratio follows.

Internal interactions merit checks when they are themselves obligations, such as avoiding a prohibited external write. Performance, concurrency and resource safety can require benchmarks, stress checks, static analysis or direct reasoning beyond ordinary output assertions.

A reversible prose-only correction may need link or reading checks rather than new executable tests. Existing adequate coverage can be rerun without adding a redundant test. This entry does not prescribe test-first development for every change or claim that passing tests prove complete correctness.

## Checks

Proposed checks, untested locally:

- Name the plausible failure and the assertion or observation that would expose it; check important boundaries and failures.
- Where consequential and practical, show that a known broken case fails before relying on a regression check.
- Review failures after restructuring: distinguish broken behavior, changed setup and unnecessary implementation coupling. Record executed checks and remaining gaps.

## Evidence

Source observations, checked 2026-11-07; linked English originals accessible:

- [Andrew Trenk, Google Testing Blog](https://testing.googleblog.com/2013/08/testing-on-toilet-test-behavior-not.html), 2013-08-05, calculator example and concluding paragraphs: recommends public behavior, allowing meaningful internal checks and setup changes.
- [Martin Fowler, Refactoring](https://refactoring.com/), undated introduction: defines restructuring through behavior-preserving steps. Original author's guidance, not an empirical guarantee.
- [Fowler, Test Pyramid](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/TestPyramid.html), 2012-05-01, main text and footnote 2: motivates focused tests through cost and brittleness, with exceptions for inexpensive reliable broad tests. No local suite was evaluated.

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-CONCURRENCY-LIFETIMES.md =====

---
id: K-CODE-CONCURRENCY-LIFETIMES
title: Review asynchronous lifetime, synchronization, and shutdown as separate contracts
summary: Keeping an object alive does not synchronize its mutable state or prove that background work finishes. Review ownership across suspension, publication ordering, cancellation, completion, and cleanup independently using the actual language and runtime contracts.
kind: reference
scope: Concurrent and asynchronous API review informed by C++ shared_ptr, Rust Send/Sync and destructors, and the Go memory model
keywords: [concurrency, lifetime, cancellation, synchronization, safe publication, shared_ptr, Send, Sync, Drop, Go memory model, 동시성, 비동기 수명, 취소, 동기화, 안전한 공개, "std::shared_ptr", "std::shared_ptr::use_count", "aliasing constructor", "RAII"]
status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-07'
relations:
  requires: [K-CODE-RESOURCE-OWNERSHIP, K-CODE-API-CONTRACTS]
context:
  evidence_check: 'Primary language documentation inspected on 2026-11-07; current C++ working draft rendering, live Rust documents, Go memory model version 2022-06-06'
  application_status: 'Reference synthesis and proposed review protocol; no scheduler, race detector, cancellation, or shutdown scenario executed'
---

# Review asynchronous lifetime, synchronization, and shutdown as separate contracts

## Guidance

Review three obligations independently: valid data access, coordination of conflicting access, and shutdown with unfinished work. A local RAII wrapper does not prove that a callback or worker has stopped using its resource. Treat task ownership and cleanup order as part of the interface.

The C++ draft separates `std::shared_ptr` bookkeeping from pointed-to state for data races. Reference counting does not protect mutable fields; review the pointer variable separately from distinct owners. With aliasing constructors, shared ownership does not extend an unrelated stored target's lifetime; dereferencing it requires a valid target. `std::shared_ptr::use_count` observes the number of shared owners. A sampled count does not order accesses to the object or wait for a worker to finish.

Rust's `Send` permits ownership transfer between threads and `Sync` permits shared reference access under their trait contracts. `Rc` does not supply these concurrency guarantees; manually implementing the traits requires unsafe reasoning. The Reference also permits intentionally suppressed destruction and process termination without unwinding. Compile-time safety does not establish leak freedom, deadlock freedom, or eventual task completion.

Go's memory model specifies publication and visibility through synchronization. It explicitly says goroutine exit alone is not synchronized before another event. Use the applicable channel, lock, or atomic contract when another participant must observe an effect.

## Applicability and exceptions

These are comparisons of specific language contracts, not interchangeable guarantees. Framework task cancellation may request cooperation, discard a result, drop a future, or interrupt work; inspect the actual API. Holding an object alive does not establish thread affinity, engine validity, or permission to call every method. Unsafe code and foreign interfaces add obligations outside ordinary safe-reference reasoning.

## Checks

Proposed implementation checks:

- Trace captured references and owners across queueing, suspension, callbacks, retries, and owner destruction. Name the completion event that permits cleanup.
- Draw the publication and conflicting-access ordering; identify its channel, mutex, atomic, or documented executor guarantee. Review lock ordering and waits separately.
- Exercise cancellation before start, during work, and during teardown. Verify whether completion is awaited, who releases resources, and whether callbacks can arrive afterward.
- Reproduce shutdown under contention with available race diagnostics and bounded completion assertions. A passing run samples schedules; it is not a proof of every interleaving.

## Evidence

Source observations checked on 2026-11-07:

- [C++ working draft: shared_ptr](https://eel.is/c++draft/util.smartptr.shared): [util.smartptr.shared.general] paragraphs 1 and 5, aliasing-constructor note 1, and use_count observer notes; mutable draft rendering, not a pinned published standard.
- [Rust Book: Send and Sync](https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch16-04-extensible-concurrency-sync-and-send.html): transfer, shared access, and manual implementation sections.
- [Rust Reference: destructors](https://doc.rust-lang.org/reference/destructors.html): Drop scopes and Not running destructors; live Reference.
- [Go memory model](https://go.dev/ref/mem): June 6, 2022 version; Advice, Goroutine destruction, and Channel communication.

All cited text was readable. No runtime safety or liveness check was executed; the review protocol remains proposed.

status: "active"

---

# Separate invariant constants, tunable policy, deployment config and secrets

## Guidance

Source observations: C++ Core Guidelines ES.45 favors meaningful symbolic constants over unexplained literals [S1]. Twelve-Factor distinguishes deploy-varying configuration from internal application structure [S2]. In Unreal, `UDataTable` stores named rows with a reflected schema derived from `FTableRowBase`. `UCurveTable` stores named numeric curves evaluated at an input value. Compare a row's units, valid range and missing-row policy with the caller; for a curve also check interpolation and the input domain [S3]. OWASP treats secrets as an access and lifecycle concern, not merely a file-location choice [S4].

Proposed method: classify each value as a domain/protocol invariant, a product policy or tuning value, environment-specific configuration, a secret, or example/test data. Identify who changes it, how often, and whether changing it requires a build, content release, deployment or runtime reload. Use the simplest representation supporting that lifecycle.

For configurable values, define type, unit, valid range, required/default behavior, precedence and compatibility. Validate at the input boundary and make the effective nonsecret configuration inspectable. Treat reload as a state transition with consistency and failure behavior; avoid spreading reads of mutable global settings through business logic.

## Applicability and exceptions

A literal is not automatically a defect. Small obvious values, specification constants and explicit test vectors can be clearest in code. Externalizing every number adds schema, distribution and compatibility costs. Environment variables are not a universal game-content format or sufficient secret protection. A table changes values, not necessarily behavior or executable policy; keep authority and validation explicit.

## Checks

Proposed checks: can the intended owner change one policy without editing unrelated logic? Are invalid, missing and conflicting values handled deliberately? Can a release reproduce its effective settings without exposing credentials? Check content/configuration revisions and tests at the actual boundary; a constant-to-file rewrite alone does not prove maintainability.

## Evidence

- [S1] [C++ Core Guidelines](https://isocpp.github.io/CppCoreGuidelines/CppCoreGuidelines#Res-magic), ES.45, Reason/Example/Enforcement; only this rule used here.
- [S2] [Twelve-Factor: Config](https://www.12factor.net/config), deploy variation and exclusion of internal application configuration.
- [S3] Epic, [Data Driven Gameplay Elements](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/data-driven-gameplay-elements-in-unreal-engine), DataTables/CurveTables and authored data workflow; UE5.8 documentation at inspection.
- [S4] OWASP, [Secrets Management](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Secrets_Management_Cheat_Sheet.html), Introduction / General Secrets Management / Secret Lifecycle.
- Primary official text sections checked 2026-11-07. Proposed classification/checks were not applied to project code or secrets in this task.

status: "active"

context: {"evidence_check": "Named original author papers or official text sections inspected; specific limits in Evidence", "application_status": "Source-based reference and proposed synthesis; no local implementation or runtime validation"}

# Represent valid data states explicitly and validate transitions

## Guidance

Source observations: Minsky replaces a connection-state record with state-specific payloads in his OCaml example [S1]. The Rust book uses enum variants and Option to distinguish alternatives and presence [S2], then shows private representation with a validated constructor [S3]. These are representational techniques, not proofs that all business rules hold.

Proposed method: list the states that actually belong to the domain, what data each requires, and permitted transitions. Avoid unrelated boolean flags and optional fields when they admit impossible combinations. Introduce a focused value type when it removes repeated interpretation or validation, not merely to wrap every primitive.

Keep construction, parsing, deserialization and mutation on the same invariant boundary. Decide how invalid external input is reported and where recovery belongs. Track units and identity where confusion is costly. Keep state transitions observable through the public contract so callers need not remember a hidden sequence of setters.

## Applicability and exceptions

Types cannot establish rules that were omitted, modeled incorrectly or bypassed through public mutation, reflection, unsafe code or an unchecked serializer. An unsigned type does not establish a business range. Deliberate intermediate states may be valid during a transaction or editor operation; model them rather than forbidding legitimate work. Choose exceptions, error results and panic/assert behavior under the actual language contract.

## Checks

Proposed checks: can invalid combinations be constructed through any supported entry point? Do transitions preserve required data and reject invalid input at the correct boundary? Review round-trip serialization, upgrades and mutation, not only the happy-path constructor. Verify claimed static guarantees with the actual compiler and runtime; no such exercise was run here.

## Evidence

- [S1] Yaron Minsky, [Effective ML Revisited](https://blog.janestreet.com/effective-ml-revisited/), 2011, Make illegal states unrepresentable; published examples read, linked lecture not watched.
- [S2] [The Rust Programming Language: Defining an Enum](https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch06-01-defining-an-enum.html), variant payloads / Option.
- [S3] [To panic! or Not to panic!](https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/ch09-03-to-panic-or-not-to-panic.html), Custom Types for Validation, including visibility and construction.
- Original author/official text checked 2026-11-07. Checks are a proposed synthesis, not a project compiler or serialization test.

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-DEPENDENCY-DIRECTION.md =====

id: K-CODE-DEPENDENCY-DIRECTION
title: Direct dependencies toward contracts that protect policy
summary: Protect important policy from volatile implementation details with a suitable contract, while avoiding interfaces that merely duplicate stable concrete APIs.
kind: reference
scope: Dependency design across software policy and implementation boundaries; C++ historical DIP and Go interface examples require language-specific adaptation
keywords: [dependency inversion, DIP, dependency direction, coupling, policy, interface ownership, dependency injection, 의존성 역전, 의존 방향, 결합도, 정책, 인터페이스]
status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-07'
context:
  evidence_check: Martin original DIP article and official Go and Google guidance inspected on 2026-11-07
  application_status: Reference only; no dependency graph or runtime behavior verified in a target implementation
---

# Direct dependencies toward contracts that protect policy

## Guidance

Source observation: Martin's 1996 DIP article argues that policy and implementation should depend on abstractions, and abstractions should avoid implementation details. Effective Go shows behavior-based interfaces; Google's build chapter distinguishes explicit direct dependencies from accidental transitive ones. These discuss different dependency mechanisms.

Synthesis: identify the policy worth preserving and the detail likely to vary, then choose a contract expressing the policy's needs. Avoid defining it through vendor types, database rows, transport failures, or configuration unless intentionally required. Centralize construction and concrete selection where composition is understood.

Draw source, build, and runtime relationships separately. Calling an adapter at runtime does not require policy source to import its concrete implementation. Conversely, passing an interface does not remove dependence on its semantics, lifetime, ordering, or shared state. Evaluate change propagation and knowledge requirements as well as import counts. One import of a volatile shared schema can matter more than several stable library imports.

## Applicability and exceptions

Use inversion for a substitution boundary, external integration, expensive side effect, or independently evolving policy. A useful seam may have one production implementation. Several implementations need not share an abstraction when their contracts differ.

Do not require an interface for every class, a dependency injection container, or two adapters at every boundary. Stable concrete types or internal helpers may need no indirection. Parameters, callbacks, protocols, traits, or standard contracts may suffice. Martin's C++ examples are historical arguments; Go's implicit interface satisfaction is language-specific. Framework ownership and generated interfaces can constrain placement.

## Checks

Proposed checks for the chosen implementation:

- Write the policy's required operations and guarantees before selecting an adapter API.
- Inspect source and build graphs for forbidden concrete imports, cycles, and undeclared direct dependencies.
- Try one credible detail change on paper; locate policy edits, including leaked data and error types.
- Exercise contract behavior with the actual adapter where applicable; a test double alone cannot establish integration compatibility.
- Remove an interface mentally: if no volatility, knowledge, or testing cost returns, reconsider its value.

## Evidence

- [Martin, The Dependency Inversion Principle](https://www.cs.utexas.edu/users/downing/papers/DIP-1996.pdf), pp. 5–7 and 10–11: Copy, layers, and adapter discussion; original article inspected through a university mirror.
- [Effective Go](https://go.dev/doc/effective_go#interfaces), “Interfaces and other types”; behavior contracts inspected. Page warns its 2009 guidance is not actively updated.
- [Software Engineering at Google, chapter 18](https://abseil.io/resources/swe-book/html/ch18.html), “Internal dependencies”; build-specific advice inspected. No local architecture or performance result was established.

status: active

  evidence_check: Russ Cox primary essay and official Go module guidance inspected on 2026-11-07
  application_status: Reference only; no package audit, vulnerability scan, installation, or integration test performed
---

# Select dependencies by lifecycle value and ownership cost

## Guidance

Source observation: Cox treats reuse as outsourced engineering with continuing risks and recommends inspecting, testing, isolating, and revisiting dependencies. Go documentation provides module inventory, update, and cleanup workflows; its version guidance distinguishes compatibility signals and unstable development releases.

Synthesis: compare existing project facilities, the standard library, mature external packages, and small local implementations against the capability needed. Weigh expertise and edge cases supplied by reuse against continuing commitments: platform support, transitive graph, build and deployment weight, licensing fit, maintenance, upgrades, and removal. Fewer direct declarations can conceal a larger transitive surface.

Prefer a package whose supported behavior closely fits the need over an oversized framework adopted for a trivial convenience. Conversely, rewriting a mature parser, codec, or security-sensitive primitive can create a much larger maintenance obligation than retaining a well-supported dependency. The goal is justified commitments, not zero packages.

For Go, the documented workflow includes discovering updates with `go list` and synchronizing declared requirements with `go mod tidy`. Go's version guidance distinguishes stable releases from development and prerelease versions without stability guarantees. Synthesis: assign maintenance responsibility and review relevant behavior before upgrading; equivalent operations differ across ecosystems.

## Applicability and exceptions

Apply before adding a package and when its support, trust, requirements, or platform assumptions change. Small, easily understood functionality may be cheaper to own locally. Copying or vendoring still transfers bug-fix and update work and must preserve required notices.

Regulated, offline, embedded, or constrained distribution environments can change the tradeoff. Compatibility versioning communicates intended promises; it does not prove correctness or suitability. Go examples are not instructions for other package managers. Security and license decisions require current package-specific evidence; this entry certifies no package.

## Checks

Proposed adoption and maintenance checks:

- Record required capability, considered alternatives, selected version, and why its total cost is acceptable.
- Inspect direct and transitive dependencies, supported targets, license, release history, and maintenance ownership.
- Exercise representative inputs, failure behavior, and important integration constraints before adoption.
- Define update, rollback, and replacement paths; verify relevant behavior again after material upgrades.

## Evidence

- [Cox, Our Software Dependency Problem](https://research.swtch.com/deps), “What is a dependency?”, “Inspect,” “Abstract,” “Avoid,” and “Upgrade”; 2019 author essay inspected. Examples are historical, not current package ratings.
- [Go: Managing dependencies](https://go.dev/doc/modules/managing-dependencies), workflow, discovering updates, and synchronizing dependencies; official commands inspected, not executed.
- [Go: Module version numbering](https://go.dev/doc/modules/version-numbers), development, prerelease, and major/minor/patch sections; official compatibility conventions inspected. No local runtime result follows from these readings.

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-GC-LIFETIMES.md =====

---
id: K-CODE-GC-LIFETIMES
title: Separate garbage collection, resource cleanup, and memory diagnosis
summary: Garbage collection reclaims eligible unreachable managed objects, while useful lifetime and prompt resource cleanup still need explicit design. Diagnose growing memory with reachability, allocation, and fragmentation evidence before choosing a fix.
kind: reference
scope: .NET managed-memory diagnosis and Unreal UObject references; source-specific mechanisms rather than guarantees for every garbage collector
keywords: [garbage collection, GC, reachability, retention, memory leak, fragmentation, allocation churn, IDisposable, UObject, TObjectPtr, 메모리 관리, 가비지 컬렉션, 메모리 누수, 객체 보존, "UPROPERTY", "TStrongObjectPtr", "TWeakObjectPtr", "TSoftObjectPtr"]
status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-07'
context:
  evidence_check: 'Microsoft Learn and Epic primary documentation inspected on 2026-11-07; Epic served Unreal Engine 5.8'
  application_status: 'Reference synthesis; no target process, heap dump, profiler session, or resource-cleanup test executed'
---

# Separate garbage collection, resource cleanup, and memory diagnosis

## Guidance

Distinguish three questions: whether an object remains reachable, whether the application still needs it, and whether an external resource must close now. In .NET, GC discovers unreachable managed objects from roots and reclaims eligible memory during collections. It cannot infer that a reachable cache entry is useless, and it does not release every kind of memory or native handle.

Use the documented disposal contract for owned disposable resources. Microsoft distinguishes `Dispose` cleanup from reclaiming the managed object's storage. An object can be disposed yet remain reachable; ownership determines whether a containing object should cascade disposal. Prompt file or socket closure needs an explicit resource protocol rather than reliance on collection timing.

For Unreal, examine the actual `UObject` reference kind. Epic's current table distinguishes strong, weak, and soft references; `TObjectPtr` is GC-safe only when marked `UPROPERTY`. `UPROPERTY` marks a reflected member so a reachable owner can expose that strong reference to GC. `TStrongObjectPtr` holds a strong GC reference even outside reflected fields. `TWeakObjectPtr` observes a loaded object without keeping it alive and must be checked before use. `TSoftObjectPtr` can identify an asset by path without loading it or retaining the loaded object. A pointer-shaped value alone does not establish engine-tracked reachability.

## Applicability and exceptions

Apply the .NET statements to CLR-managed objects and the Epic statements to the documented engine version. Native allocations, GPU resources, allocator caches, and engine subsystems require their own accounting. GC eligibility is not a promise of immediate reclamation or immediate reduction in process memory. Collection policies differ across runtimes; do not translate the exact .NET algorithm into an Unreal guarantee.

## Checks

Proposed diagnostic sequence:

- Reproduce a fixed workload and compare settled baselines. Separate outstanding allocations with lost ownership, reachable-but-unwanted retention, fragmented free space, and rapid temporary allocation churn; growth alone does not identify which occurred.
- Compare managed heap size, allocation rate, collection behavior, and process/native memory. Inspect dominant types and root paths; Microsoft's tutorial uses counters, dumps, `dumpheap`, and `gcroot`.
- For suspected retention, inspect cache limits, subscriptions, and task captures. For resource exhaustion, count live handles and verify disposal separately. For Unreal, inspect reflected reference tracking and unintended strong references.

## Evidence

Source observations checked on 2026-11-07:

- [Microsoft: GC fundamentals](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/standard/garbage-collection/fundamentals): Memory release, Unmanaged resources, and fragmentation discussion.
- [Microsoft: implement Dispose](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/standard/garbage-collection/implementing-dispose): introduction, Cascade dispose calls, and Dispose() versus managed-storage release.
- [Microsoft: diagnose a leak](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/core/diagnostics/debug-memory-leak): Examine managed memory usage and Analyze the core dump; tutorial applies to .NET Core 3.1 onward, tool output varies.
- [Epic: object pointers](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/en-us/unreal-engine/object-pointers-in-unreal-engine): pointer table footnotes, TObjectPtr, and TStrongObjectPtr; UE 5.8.

Microsoft pages displayed authorization notices but exposed the cited body text. No local measurements were taken; proposed checks establish no diagnosis or verified safeguard here.

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-GIT-ISOLATED-MERGE.md =====

---
id: K-CODE-GIT-ISOLATED-MERGE
title: Resolve a large branch merge in a sparse linked worktree without touching the main checkout
summary: Dry-run the merge with git merge-tree to get the conflict list and stage blobs, then do the real merge in a linked worktree with a cone sparse checkout of only the conflicting directories and LFS smudge disabled; decide binary and generated conflicts from history and regeneration, and keep EOL-only regeneration drift out of the index.
kind: method
scope: Git repositories with large binary or LFS content where the main working copy must stay untouched (open editors, locked files, no checkout rule) while a merge with many conflicts is resolved
keywords: [git merge, git merge-tree, git worktree, sparse-checkout, GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE, LFS pointer conflict, binary conflict, generated code, protoc, core.autocrlf, EOL drift, MERGE_MSG, cleanup=verbatim, merge commit]
status: active
checked_at: "2026-11-06"
context:
  vcs: git 2.x with git-lfs
  platform: Windows (core.autocrlf=true)
---

## Guidance

1. Dry run first: `git merge-tree --write-tree --name-only <ours> <theirs>` lists the conflicted paths without updating the index or working tree, and the full output gives the base, ours and theirs blob ids per path. Save both; they are the ground truth for the rest of the work.
2. Create the merge branch in a linked worktree: `git worktree add -b <merge-branch> <dir> <ours>`, then `git sparse-checkout set --cone <conflict dirs>` so only the directories holding conflicts (plus what regeneration tools need) are materialized. Export `GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE=1` for every git command there so LFS files stay pointers.
3. Run `git merge --no-ff --no-commit <theirs>` in the worktree. The conflict set must equal the dry run; stop if it does not.
4. Decide each binary or LFS conflict from history, never by default to a side: compare the two sides' blob ids at the commits that changed the path (`git log <base>..<side> -- <path>`, `git rev-parse <commit>:<path>`). If a side's blob is identical to a known historical version, inspect the later history and intended changes before choosing the staged version. Two unrelated changes to one asset stay a human decision; list them with both sides' tickets, authors and dates.
5. Regenerate generated outputs (protobuf code, compiled data) from the auto-merged source instead of merging them. Before trusting the generator, regenerate each side's committed outputs from that side's own sources and require equality ignoring line endings.
6. With `core.autocrlf=true`, a generator that writes LF makes every output look modified while the normalized diff is empty. Classify with `git diff --quiet -- <file>` (normalized) per file and restore the EOL-only files with `git checkout -- <file>`; stage only files whose normalized content changed. A generator that enumerates inputs with an unordered directory listing can also reorder whole blocks: compare added and removed lines to locate a pure reorder, then establish that the language and generator contract allow it before discarding the difference.
7. Write the merge commit message into `.git/worktrees/<name>/MERGE_MSG` (or `.git/MERGE_MSG`): keep git's subject and the `# Conflicts:` block verbatim and append per-path resolution notes; commit with `git commit --cleanup=verbatim` so the block survives. Keep human decisions visibly unresolved in the index and finish them before making the merge commit.
8. Before the commit: no unmerged paths, `git status` shows no unstaged drift in the worktree, and every regenerated or semantically merged artifact has an independent check recorded.

## Applicability and exceptions

Applies to merges where the number or size of conflicts makes an in-place merge in the main checkout unsafe. Sparse checkout must include every directory a regeneration tool reads or writes, otherwise the tool runs on a partial tree; extend the sparse set before running it. An LFS conflict appears as a text conflict on the pointer lines when no `merge.lfs` driver is configured; the pointer is resolved by `git checkout --ours|--theirs -- <path>` followed by `git add`, not by editing it. Semantic merges of data files have their own methods (see K-CODE-UE-LOC-MERGE for Unreal localization files).

## Checks

- Dry-run conflict list == real merge conflict list.
- For every binary path resolved to a side, the index blob equals that side's blob (`git ls-files -s`).
- For every regenerated output, regeneration from the staged sources reproduces the staged blob ignoring CR.
- `git status --short` in the worktree shows only `UU` for the deliberately deferred paths and `M `/`A ` for staged entries; nothing in ` M`, `MM` or `??` after regeneration.
- The main checkout's `git status` is unchanged by the whole procedure.

## Evidence

In a UE5 game repository on Windows on 2026-11-06 the preview and real merge identified the same paths. Binary history and independent regeneration distinguished genuine differences from line-ending and order-only changes. Unrelated edits to the same binary were left for human resolution. Custom LFS merge drivers and Git versions predating `merge-tree --write-tree` (2.38) were not tested. [Git, merge-tree](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-merge-tree/2.45.0.html) · [Git, worktree](https://git-scm.com/docs/git-worktree)

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-INFORMATION-HIDING.md =====

---
id: K-CODE-INFORMATION-HIDING
title: Place module boundaries around hidden design decisions
summary: Group responsibilities around decisions that should change privately, and evaluate a boundary by what callers must know rather than by file or class size.
kind: reference
scope: Software module design and refactoring; historical information-hiding arguments adapted to current language and performance constraints
keywords: [information hiding, encapsulation, modularity, cohesion, temporal decomposition, change propagation, 정보 은닉, 캡슐화, 모듈성, 응집도, 변경 전파]
status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-07'
context:
  evidence_check: Primary texts and official C++ guidance inspected on 2026-11-07; source observations separated from design synthesis
  application_status: Reference only; no project refactoring or runtime verification performed
---

# Place module boundaries around hidden design decisions

## Guidance

Source observation: Parnas contrasts processing-stage decomposition with boundaries that conceal changeable decisions. His module is a responsibility assignment, not necessarily a procedure. Abseil gives a narrower C++ example: file-local helpers can restrict visibility and make inputs clearer, with exceptions for object-state interactions.

Synthesis: identify a decision whose change would otherwise spread: representation, persistence format, ordering policy, resource ownership, or a protocol rule. Put its interpretation and mutation behind one coherent contract. State what callers may rely on and what remains private. A module can span several files; several tiny classes can still expose one shared implementation decision.

Trace knowledge crossing the boundary. Returning an internal container, requiring initialization in a special order, or exposing storage-specific errors can couple clients even when fields are private. Prefer operations expressing the caller's purpose when they eliminate that knowledge. Keep necessary behavior visible: ownership, failure, lifetime, concurrency, and ordering guarantees belong in the contract when callers need them.

## Applicability and exceptions

Use this when changes repeatedly cross module boundaries or reviewers must understand many internals together. Processing stages describe execution, streaming, or scheduling; they become useful maintenance boundaries when their shared contract is stable and independently useful.

Do not hide every implementation fact through another layer. A public data format may be the intended contract. Performance-sensitive boundaries may need batching, value types, or inlining; Parnas explicitly discusses implementation overhead. Neither his historical examples nor Abseil's C++ default establishes a universal module size or language-independent helper rule.

## Checks

Proposed review checks, not demonstrated safeguards:

- Name the decision hidden by each candidate module and the clients that currently know it.
- Walk through a plausible representation or policy change; list required client edits and explain each contractual dependency.
- Review exposed types, mutable aliases, call order, errors, and shared state for knowledge leaks.
- Confirm clients can perform normal work without reconstructing internals; measure actual hot paths before accepting performance claims.

## Evidence

- [Parnas, 1972 primary text](https://www.cs.lafayette.edu/~gexia/cs301/resources/parnas.html), “Changeability,” “The Criteria,” and “Efficiency and Implementation”; university transcription inspected, with its explicit accuracy warning. ACM publisher PDF returned 403.
- [Original 1971 CMU report scan](https://prl.khoury.northeastern.edu/img/p-tr-1971.pdf), title and “The Criteria,” printed pp. 17–21; repository cover, university mirror. Earlier report cross-check, not the 1972 edition.
- [Abseil Tip 186](https://abseil.io/tips/186), “Benefits” and “Reasons to Look Elsewhere”; C++ advice inspected. These sources supply design reasoning, not measured results for this repository.

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-MAINTAINABILITY-EVIDENCE.md =====

---
id: "K-CODE-MAINTAINABILITY-EVIDENCE"
title: "Judge maintainability through concrete changes and multiple outcomes"
summary: "Evaluate a design or refactor with representative understanding and modification tasks, preserved behavior and change impact. Treat size, complexity and coupling metrics as evidence dimensions rather than a single proof of maintainability."
kind: "reference"
scope: "Maintainability assessment and refactoring decisions; observational Windows study and a bounded student Java experiment do not establish universal design laws"
keywords: ["maintainability", "refactoring", "empirical", "change-impact", "modularity", "comprehension", "metrics", "유지보수성", "리팩터링", "실증연구", "변경영향", "이해도", "지표"]
status: "active"
checked_at: "2026-11-07"
context: {"evidence_check": "Named original author papers or official text sections inspected; specific limits in Evidence", "application_status": "Source-based reference and proposed synthesis; no local implementation or runtime validation"}
---

# Judge maintainability through concrete changes and multiple outcomes

## Guidance

Source observations: Kim, Zimmermann and Nagappan studied Microsoft refactoring through surveys, interviews and Windows history; dependency/complexity reductions coexisted with size growth, and the paper explicitly limits causal conclusions [S1]. Tempero, Blincoe and Lottridge compared two Java designs with 40 students: modification success favored higher modularity, while the lower comprehension result was a statistical trend, not an established general effect [S2].

Proposed method: identify a representative future change and the behavior that must survive. Trace which sources, decisions, dependencies and tests a maintainer must understand or modify. Compare an existing and proposed structure on correctness, effort, locality, build/runtime cost and explanation burden. Include a second kind of change when it could expose a hidden tradeoff.

Use code metrics to locate questions, then inspect actual work and evidence. Prefer a bounded experiment or change walkthrough when the structural benefit is uncertain. Distinguish a reasoned prediction, a completed modification and longer-term defect or productivity evidence.

## Applicability and exceptions

The Windows study concerns a particular large refactoring effort and reports correlation, not isolated causation. The student experiment used a limited task and comprehension questions; the understanding trend had p=.07. Neither establishes an ideal class count or a blanket preference for more indirection. Changes in team experience, tooling and task distribution can alter results.

## Checks

Proposed checks: state a falsifiable maintenance benefit and preserve the original functional contract. Inspect changed callers and integration obligations, not only local code. Can a maintainer explain why the representation exists and where the next change belongs? Report observed results and limits without claiming fewer lines, more interfaces or a score alone establishes better software.

## Evidence

- [S1] Kim, Zimmermann and Nagappan, [An Empirical Study of Refactoring Challenges and Benefits at Microsoft](https://web.cs.ucla.edu/~miryung/Publications/tse2014-refactoringpractices.pdf), IEEE TSE 2014. Author-hosted full PDF; Abstract, discussion around Tables 5-7, and section 5 Threats to Validity inspected (PDF pp. 1,12-14).
- [S2] Tempero, Blincoe and Lottridge, [An Experiment on the Effects of Modularity on Code Modification and Understanding](https://kblincoe.github.io/publications/2023_ACE_Modularity.pdf), ACE2023. Author-hosted full PDF; sections 3.3, 4, 5 and 5.1 inspected (PDF pp. 5-7).
- Checked 2026-11-07. The assessment procedure is a synthesis; no new participant study, refactoring or maintenance-task experiment was run.

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-PERFORMANCE-MEASUREMENT.md =====

---
id: K-CODE-PERFORMANCE-MEASUREMENT
title: Measure representative workloads before trading clarity for optimization
summary: Set relevant latency, throughput and memory budgets, characterize the workload
  and locate the limiting work before optimizing. Check that a benchmark measures
  the intended operation and preserve correctness and maintainability alongside measured
  gains.
kind: reference
scope: Application and systems performance decisions including game frame-time and
  allocation costs; tools and measurement interpretation remain runtime-specific
keywords: ["profiling","benchmark","latency","throughput","goodput","tail latency","p95","frame pacing","fixed timestep","token bucket","FIFO","backpressure","Unreal Insights","Timing Insights","occlusion query","Render Hardware Interface","Present","binary heap","snapshot lifetime","working set","private commit","benchmark::DoNotOptimize","성능","프로파일링","프레임 시간","비동기 저장"]
status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-11'
context:
  engine: "Historical UE 5.8.3 Windows D3D11 real-time packaged workloads and separate PIE behavior checks"
  evidence: "Methodology survey and bounded frame, planner, queue and completed-simulation save comparisons; no production guarantee"
---

## Guidance

Define the user operation, workload, correctness contract and budget first. Separate latency for an individual request, throughput per unit time, allocation rate, retained memory and peak process memory. Inspect the system during the benchmark to verify what limits it. In a microbenchmark, consume results and inspect generated work where necessary. `benchmark::DoNotOptimize` helps keep a result observable but still permits optimization of its expression.

**Pooling and caching.** Object pooling (메모리 풀링) reuses allocated storage or object instances. Compare creation and reset costs, allocation rate, retained capacity, cache locality and tail frame time under the same live population. Include pool warmup, release and reacquisition. A lower allocation count can coexist with higher retained memory or stale state. Caching, batching and concurrency likewise have invalidation, latency or contention costs; repeat the end-to-end benchmark after any local improvement.

**Frame time.** In Unreal Insights, use Timing Insights to select a complete frame and inspect Game, Render, Render Hardware Interface (RHI) and available GPU tracks. A callback measures only its own start-to-end interval. Start-to-start cadence also includes time between callbacks. Align work with the named frame and the following cadence interval, retain startup, warmup, steady state, observer overhead, final save and exit boundaries. Clip and union nested or concurrent timer spans before attributing elapsed time. A CPU wait in `Present` identifies a blocked thread. GPU work, display pacing and driver causes require separate evidence. Toggle an occlusion query only as a diagnostic in the same scene and check visibility and GPU cost before keeping the change.

**Equal work.** Compare interleaved runs of the same executable, viewport, rendering settings, data and input schedule. Preserve frame counts, percentiles, maxima and deadline-miss counts per condition. A passing p95 allows individual deadline misses. For a fixed timestep of duration h, verify each row satisfies previous backlog + elapsed wall time − steps × h = new backlog, including any explicitly discarded time as a separate term. Reconstruct arrivals, queue depth, oldest request and each consumer's lifetime. A token bucket allows bursts from accumulated credits and limits admission over time. A FIFO queue admits the oldest eligible item first. Report latency among accepted requests alongside refusals and on-time goodput (original requests completed on time per unit time) at the same offered load. An age measured at spawn omits time spent waiting for admission.

**Algorithm and recording changes.** With a binary heap, precomputed packed priority keys can reduce comparator work. Check representable bit ranges, tie order, per-request paths and expansion counts, then measure the memory added per planner. Fine-grained timers can perturb short calls. Use them for attribution and repeat with coarse tracing and tracing disabled. For recording changes, measure snapshot capture, serialization, write, cleanup, publication-to-acknowledgement and launch-to-exit time. Compare complete output and ownership after the input is changed or destroyed. Backpressure limits accepted work when a writer falls behind. Include active and queued inputs in its byte budget, and measure the producer, encoding buffers and retained history separately. Private commit measures process-private committed memory on Windows. Working set measures resident pages. Periodic samples can miss short-lived peaks.

## Applicability and exceptions

These checks apply to comparable loads with an observable result and trace boundary. A synthetic scene, static route graph, fixed camera, accelerated capture, Debug binary or editor session cannot establish a packaged game's frame deadlines. A bounded queue leaves other memory consumers unbounded. Capacity, latency, fairness, dynamic topology changes, live autosaves and driver-level causes each need evidence in the target workload.

## Checks

- Run the baseline and candidate in alternating order, retaining each denominator, individual missed deadlines and where callbacks cannot see work. Use the original input IDs and identical simulation epochs to detect missing or reordered work.
- Challenge the verifier with a dropped sample, corrupted intermediate counter, wrong executable identity or output with altered order or values. Match build identity against the artifact actually launched.
- For background saves, separate game-thread submission and receipt, worker time, draining and process shutdown. Exercise queue-full, write failure and shutdown with accepted work. A buffered close and rename alone do not establish crash durability.

## Evidence

The primary methodology survey dates to 2026-11-07. Historical UE 5.8.3 Windows/D3D11 comparisons on 2026-11-09–11 exercised the frame, queue, planner and save checks above. They observed deadline misses despite acceptable p95, rendering waits outside planner work, and different capture and finalization costs. The save comparison covered an already-finished simulation. Registered report IDs identify historical evidence. This review checked Epic's profiler description and did not rerun the workloads.

- [Brendan Gregg, Performance Analysis Methodology](https://www.brendangregg.com/methodology.html) · [Active Benchmarking](https://www.brendangregg.com/activebenchmarking.html)
- [Google Benchmark User Guide](https://google.github.io/benchmark/user_guide.html) · [Epic, Timing Insights](https://dev.epicgames.com/documentation/unreal-engine/timing-insights-in-unreal-engine?application_version=5.8)
- [Frame and world checks](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-editor-gate-results-md) · [Variable frame workloads](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-dynamic-results-md) · [Per-consumer checks](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-traffic-results-md)
- [Sustained load](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-stream-results-md) · [Admission](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-admission-results-md) · [Frame attribution](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-hitch-results-md) · [Planner comparison](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-planner-results-md)
- [Snapshot ownership](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-snapshot-results-md) · [Asynchronous publication](../sources.md#src-ue-lab-async-save-results-md)

===== knowledge/code/K-CODE-PRINCIPLE-REUSE.md =====

status: active
checked_at: '2026-11-07'
context:
  authority: User request to collect programming philosophy knowledge in the ongoing reusable knowledge workflow

  application_status: Knowledge retrieval and proposed review checks; no repository code refactor, compiler test or runtime experiment
